CHAPTER I INTRODUCTION This Ninth Semiannual Report of the Small Business 

Administration provides detailed information on the progress of the Agency in carrying out these responsibilities assigned to it by Congress: 

1. Helping small business gain access to adequate capital and credit; 

2. Helping small business obtain a fair share of Government procurement; 

3. Helping small business obtain competent management, technical, and production counsel; and 

4. Making disaster loans to persons whose homes or businesses have been damaged by storms, floods, and other disasters, and to small business concerns which have suffered substantial economic injury because of drought conditions in their areas.

In addition, this report reviews various aspects of the changing economic position of small business in our economy. 

Many activities of this Agency during the past 6-months period have been conducted in co-operation with other Government agencies, with the Small Business Administration serving as a spokesman and advocate of small business. 

The Administrator, in addition to serving as chairman of the Small Business Administration's Loan Policy Board, is a 1

2 member of the President's Cabinet Committee on Small Business, the Defense Mobilization Board, and the Committee for Rural Development Program. 

The Small Business Administration is also represented on the following interagency committees:

President's Cabinet Committee on Small Business: 

Subcommittee on Financing 

Subcommittee on Labor

Subcommittee on Urban Renewal

Policies and Procedures

Committee on Procurement Size Standards United States Civil Service Commission's Interagency Advisory Group Specific examples of Small Business Administration collaboration with other agencies follow:

This Agency has worked closely with the Securities and Exchange Commission on ways and means of making equity capital more readily available to small business. In November the Agency arranged a conference with leaders in the business and investment community and officials of the Securities and Exchange Commission. 

Out of the discussions came many proposals for statutory and administrative changes to benefit small business. These proposals are now under consideration.

Representatives of this Agency have conferred with representatives of the Civil Aeronautics Administration about financial assistance to small airlines, including loans to private airports for hangar facilities.

The Agency has discussed with members of the Federal Communications Commission the question of subscription television and its impact on small business concerns. Close liaison has been continued with the Department of Agriculture on the drought programs of both the Small Business 

Administration and the Department of Agriculture. 

The Agency has continued co-operation with the officials of the Fish and Wildlife Service of the Department of the Interior in establishing and administering its Fisheries Loan Program. 

Loan applications under this program are accepted and processed by the Small Business Administration.

The Small Business Administration has established a continuing liaison with the Antitrust Division of the Department of Justice through which the Agency has obtained lists of inventions and patents made available for licensing to the public through judgments in antitrust cases. The Agency has published abstracts of these patents in the monthly Products List Circular distributed to small business concerns. Officials of this Agency have been working closely with the Department of Justice in both the development of antitrust legislation and the handling of specific complaints from small business under the antitrust laws. 

The Agency is collaborating with the Business and 

Defense Services Administration of the Department of Commerce in helping small business concerns obtain access to scarce materials.

In the Disaster Loan Program the Small Business Ad ministration has worked in close co-operation with the Federal 

Civil Defense Administration, the Housing and Home Finance 

Agency and the American Red Cross to provide assistance to the victims of disasters.

Conferences have been held with officials of the Patent Office regarding fees for the filing of patents and general Government patent policy.

Conferences were held with the Bureau of Public Roads in the Department of Commerce regarding small business participation in the President's highway program. 

The Agency has conferred with the Bureau of Foreign Commerce in the Department of Commerce in regard to the effect of high tariffs on small business concerns and has brought to the attention of the United States Tariff Commission the effect on small businesses of increases in tariffs. 

Officials of the Small Business Administration have been working with officials of the Department of Health, Education and Welfare in the development of health insurance programs to be set up by small insurance companies, as well as a loan program for private hospitals, convalescent homes and clinics.

LO The Agency continued to confer with representatives of the Department of Labor in regard to amendments to existing labor laws which might improve the labor-management relations of small business concerns.

The financial and competitive problems confronting small bus and truck lines have been discussed with the Interstate 

Commerce Commission. 

Many individual cases relating to unfair trade practices have been referred to the attention of the Federal Trade Commission both as individual matters and problems of general policy. 

Recommendations have been made to the Department of State in relation to the hardships imposed on small business due to exports of ferrous scrap and other materials in short supply. 

The Agency has discussed with the Department of the Treasury changes in the tax laws for the benefit of small business. 

Among Management publications issued have been several with reference to tax problems.

Officials of the Agency have discussed with the Department of the Interior problems in connection with financial assistance for park concessionaires and financing of small oil storage tanks.

Officials of this Agency have worked with members of the Federal Reserve Board on studies relating to the financing of small business.

Conferences have been held with various Departments and Bureaus, including the Department of Defense and the Bureau of the Census, on methods of improving the definition of small business. Conferences have been held with representatives of the Urban Renewal Administration to discuss possible assistance to small business concerns displaced by new housing programs. 

This Agency has worked continuously with the Council of Economic Advisers on studies of programs for assistance to

small business. It has co-operated actively with other agencies to carry out recommendations of the President's Cabinet Committee on Small Business and to develop additional programs for future implementation.

Many Federal agencies have co-operated in the Business Opportunity Meetings cosponsored by this Agency to inform small business owners and managers of opportunities to sell to the Government and to obtain subcontract work. To date, 37 such meetings have been held of which 10 were held during the 6-months period from July through December 1957. Agencies co-operating in this work include various installations of the Army, Navy, Air Force and the joint military supply agencies, as well as such Federal civilian agencies as the Department of 

Commerce, General Services Administration, Post Office Depart ment, Department of the Interior and Veterans Administration.

CHAPTER II ECONOMICS OF SMALL BUSINESS 

This chapter presents data which indicate the economic position of small business, such as number of business failures, number of new business incorporations, earnings of smaller manufacturing corporations, military prime contract awards to small business and financial factors affecting small business. 

Business Failures 

Business failures, as reported by Dun and Bradstreet, Inc., increased 8 per cent, from 12, 686 in 1956 to 13, 739 in 1957.

The sharpest increase was in construction, reflecting the decline of 8 per cent in residential housing starts. This drop in construction in turn led to increased failures among retailers of furniture, home furnishings, lumber, building materials and hardware. In the manufacturing field, failures were approximately 3 per cent higher than in 1956. The trend of the total number of business failures is shown on page 8. Annual rates of business failures are presented on the following page. Business bankruptcy cases filed, always fewer in number than over-all business failures, showed a small increase in fiscal 1957 compared with 1956.

New Business Incorporations 

The number of business incorporations is a useful indicator of business growth because a significant relationship exists between new business incorporations and new business formations. 

Further, figures on incorporations are available on a more current basis than statistics on new businesses. The usefulness of the figures on incorporations as a measure of business growth is lessened by the fact that they include both incorporations of new businesses and incorporations of established businesses which previously had been operated as proprietorships or partnerships. The postwar trend of new business incorporations is shown on the preceding page.

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION

Financial Position of Smaller Manufacturing Corporations (Continued) 

Sales

Sales of smaller corporations (those with assets under $1 million), which had shown an upward trend since the latter part of 1954, leveled off in 1957, as shown in the table on page 11 and by the chart on page 13. In the first three quarters of the year, sales of smaller corporations totaled $32, 466 million, compared with $32,671 million in the same three quarters of 1956. Sales of the larger manufacturing corporations increased from $193, 501 million to $207, 747 million. Earnings

Costs and expenses of smaller manufacturing corporations increased from 94.8 per cent of sales in the third quarter of 1956 to 95.7 per cent in the third quarter of 1957. This increase in the cost of doing business resulted in a decrease in smaller corporations' earnings both before and after taxes. In the first three quarters of 1957, earnings of smaller manufacturing corporations before taxes were $1,292 million, compared with $1,543 million in the same three quarters of 1956. Earnings after taxes of the smaller manufacturing corporations were $678 million, compared with $849 million in the comparable three quarters of 1956. On the other hand, earnings after taxes of the larger manufacturing corporations showed a slight increase $11, 230 million in the first three quarters of 1957, compared with $11, 049 million in the first three quarters of 1956. 

As shown in the table on page 12 and by the chart on page 13, earnings of smaller manufacturing corporations since 1954 have shown a substantial trend upward despite the decline from 1956 to 1957.

Rate of Return on Stockholders' Equity 

The annual rate of return on stockholders' equities decreased in the case of both the smaller and the larger manufacturing corporations. For the smaller corporations, rate of return before taxes decreased from 21.7 per cent of stockholders' equity during the first three quarters of 1956 to 18. 2 per cent during the

first three quarters of 1957.

For the larger corporations the decrease was from 22. 8 to 21.3 per cent. After taxes, the decreases were from 11.9 to 9.6 per cent and 12. 2 to 11. 5 per cent, respectively.

As shown in the table on page 14 and by the chart on page 15, the smaller manufacturing corporations' rate of return on stockholders' equity both before and after taxes was substantially higher in 1957 than in 1954 and 1955. 

Military Procurement 

During the first 4 months of fiscal year 1958, contract awards to small business were a slightly higher percentage of total military prime contract awards than in the previous year, increasing from 18. 2 to 19.0 per cent. In fiscal 1957, small business awards were at approximately the same rate as in fiscal 1956.

Although the small business percentage of military contract awards increased in the first 4 months of fiscal year 1958 as compared with the same period of fiscal 1957, the dollar volume of small business awards decreased. In the first 4 months of fiscal 1957, the dollar volume was $1, 059 million; in the first 

4 months of the current fiscal year, it was $862 million. This decrease reflects a drop in the total net value of military procurement actions with business firms for work in the United States from $5,816 million to $4, 527 million. 

It should be stated again, as in previous reports, that opportunities for small business to share in military procurement have been reduced by changes in the amount of national security expenditures, the proportion of such expenditures to the gross national product, and the composition of major defense procurement and production. Military requirements for aircraft, including associated armament and electronics, and for guided missiles, have led to a concentration of prime contracts in industries characterized by big enterprises operating big plants. 

As shown in the chart on page 19, the dollar volume of procurements set aside for small business under the Small Business Administration-Department of Defense program was greater

during the first half of fiscal 1958 than in the first half of fiscal 1957 - $358 million compared with $325 million. Likewise, the set-aside percentage of new contracts awarded to small business

Small Business Share of New and Net Procurement 

New military procurement figures which do not include cancellations, may provide better information than net procurement data for determining how military prime contracts are currently being awarded to small business. During the past 2 fiscal years the ratio of contract terminations to new military prime contract awards has fluctuated less widely than immediately after the agreement to cease hostilities in Korea, when cancellations were widespread. As a result, the trends of the small business percentages of new and net military procurement (net equals new procurement less cancellations) have been similar. In fiscal years 

1956 and 1957, the small business share of new military prime contract awards was 17 per cent; the small business share of net military prime contract awards was 19.6 and 19.8 per cent respectively. These data are shown in the tables on pages 22 and 23. 

Small Business Potential 

During the first 4 months of fiscal 1958, the small business share of the small business potential of all military procurement actions was 58.9 per cent, compared with 55 per cent during the first 4 months of fiscal 1957. This occurred because during this recent 4-months period the decrease in military procurement was relatively greater in the case of actions of $10,000 or more than in those of less than $10,000, and the small business share of the smaller actions is greater than in actions of $10,000 or more. 

Thus, the percentage of actions under $10,000, all of which are in the small business potential, rose in relation to total procurement. 

The small business potential of total procurement may decrease if subsequent purchases involve relatively more heavy items, such as aircraft and communication equipment, and research and development projects. 

Procurement by Program

The Department of Defense recently has published an analysis of military procurement actions of $10,000 or more during fiscal year 1957. The study shows net military expenditures

by program, including the percentage and dollar amount of contract awards to small business concerns. The figures show that over-all small business awards were significantly influenced by the extent to which small business shared in contracts for construction, petroleum products, subsistence and services. It highlights the fact that increases in such programs as aircraft and missiles will inevitably decrease the small business percentage. 

Small Business Share of Advertised and Negotiated Contracts Small business received a higher proportion of advertised than of negotiated military procurement in the first 4 months of fiscal year 1958. Of procurements that were within the small business potential, small business received 67. 9 per cent of those that were advertised, and 53. 1 per cent of those that were negotiated. Of all advertised procurements, small business concerns received contracts totaling $389 million, or 55.8 per cent of the total net value. Of all negotiated procurements, small business firms received contracts totaling $473 million, or 12.3 per cent of the total net value. This greater small business percentage of advertised procurement is due almost entirely to the larger proportion of advertised procurement within the small business. potential during the first quarter of fiscal year 1958. 

Changes in Concentration in Manufacturing Industries, 1947-54 

Further study of the data compiled in the 1954 Census of Manufactures shows no uniform trend toward concentration in industry. The picture varies in different industries, depending upon growth factors, technology permitting effective use of productive facilities in small business units and low capital investment requirements. Analysis of the data shows: 

1. The number of manufacturing establishments increased from 240, 807 in the 1947 Census of Manufactures to 286, 817 in the 1954 Census. 

2. Manufacturing establishments owned or controlled by single-unit corporations increased by 30,069 and those owned by single-unit noncorporate enterprises by 19, 308.

The number of establishments operated by multiunit corporations increased only slightly, from 29, 566 to 29, 823.

Total employment in the establishments operated by multiunit corporations rose from 7,831, 000 to 9, 393,000. This increase from 55 to 60 per cent of total manufacturing employment in the Nation was accompanied by an increase of total percentage of value added by manufacture from 58 to 67 per cent. The increased employment and production reflects the growth of corporations in the aircraft, electronics, and chemicals fields.

Small manufacturers improved or maintained their position in many industries. For example, in lithographing, the number of companies, mostly small, more than doubled, from 1, 380 to 2, 879, and the percentage of value of shipments accounted for by companies smaller than the 20 largest lithographing companies increased from 71 to 80 per cent.

A list of industries in which there was a significant increase in number of companies and in the share of shipments by companies smaller than the 20 largest in the industry is shown on page 25. Thus, the list indicates a strengthening of small firms in those fields.

In contrast, the competitive position of smaller manufacturers weakened significantly in many manufacturing industries. 

Each of the 14 manufacturing industries, as shown in the table on page 26, was marked by a decrease of more than 200 companies from 1947 to 1954, together with a significant decrease in the percentage of production accounted for by companies smaller than the

Financial Factors Affecting Loan Applications 

The most important event affecting the credit picture was reduction of the discount rate to 3 per cent by Federal Reserve banks in all districts commencing in mid-November. This reduction, attributed to recognition of a slackening in business conditions, was followed by a drop in almost all market rates and in yields of Treasury corporate bonds.

Demand for bank credit, which mounted steadily during the postwar years, has shown signs of receding in recent months. Outstanding business loans of weekly reporting member banks of the Federal Reserve System were $31. 8 billion at the end of December, compared to $32. 0 billion at the end of 

September. Commercial bank loans declined by $400 million in October, compared with a rise of $300 million in October 1956. It is reasonable to expect that if the business sag continues, credit will continue to ease and demand for bank loans will decline.

Spending on plant and equipment will be cut back at least 5 per cent from the record levels of 1957 and inventories at manufacturing, wholesale and retail levels have either ceased to expand or been reduced. The improvement in the bond market may mean a shift on the part of many borrowers to new bond issues in place of short-term credit. However, activity in certain sectors of the economy may call for an increase in credit. During the next year there should be increases in residential construction, public utility construction, and public construction by State and local governments. The impact that the new defense program will have on credit demand is still to be assessed, particularly since the rate of Government spending rather than the rate of new commitments is an imponderable. 

Except for the biggest borrowers with the best credit rating, bank-lending rates are usually much more laggard than many market rates. For the smaller borrowers there may be little reduction in interest rates, unless the drop in private loan applications were severe enough to touch off competition for the smaller as well as the larger loans.

Equity and Long-Term Capital for Small Business 

Corporate securities offerings during the first 9 months of 1957 totaled $9, 889 million, exceeding total offerings of $7,928 million during the first 9 months of 1956. However, a decline was evident through the year, since offerings of $3,596 million during the first quarter diminished to $2,987 million during the third quarter, as shown in the table on page 28.

25 29 50 Offerings of bonds increased to $7, 675 million in the first three quarters of 1957, compared with $6, 151 million in the same period of 1956. In equity securities, the increase was to $2, 214 million in 1957, compared with $1,777 million in 1956. A declining trend also was evident in both equity securities and bonds, with the decline greater for the former. This pattern of decline for total corporate offerings of equity securities and bonds is in contrast to the ascending trend of 1956.

Offerings of securities under Regulation A, through which the Securities and Exchange Commission provides a simplified registration procedure for issues between $100,000 and $300,000, decreased to $89 million during the first three quarters of 1957, compared with $150 million during the first three quarters of 1956. The Regulation A offerings for the 9months period constituted 0.9 per cent of total security offerings, compared with 1.9 per cent in 1956 and 3. 0 per cent in 

1955. This disappointing reduction in Regulation A issues is attributable in part to the slump in mining issues; a tightening of Securities and Exchange Commission requirements; and the effect of inflation in raising the cost of floating small issues while diminishing the value of the proceeds left for small companies after paying the higher costs. 

Development Credit Corporations 

A recent United States Department of Commerce survey has revealed a remarkable growth of State and local credit corporations. In the 10 years since close of World War II, over 

2, 100 community industrial credit corporations have been established. Today, they are to be found in every State of the 

Union, with the largest concentration occurring in the Southeastern region. Pennsylvania, Massachusetts, Missouri, Georgia, Tennessee, Minnesota, North Carolina and New York have over 

100 each. 

Thirteen States 

11 

Connecticut, Kansas, Maine, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, New York, New Hampshire, North Carolina, Rhode Island, South Dakota, Vermont, and

Wisconsin and the Territory of Hawaii have established statewide privately financed development credit corporations; 14 other 

States Alabama, Arizona, Florida, Illinois, Mississippi, Montana, New Jersey, New Mexico, North Dakota, Oregon, South

Carolina, Virginia, Washington, and West Virginia and the Territory of Alaska are considering formation of such organizations. In addition, the States of Pennsylvania, New Hampshire, Maine, and Arkansas, and the Territory of Puerto Rico have established industrial financing authorities supported entirely or in part by State funds. 455240 O-58 -3

Problems of Small Business Officials of the Small Business Administration in constant contact with small businessmen throughout the country hear daily of the problems of small business. Among the most prominent of the problems are the following: 1. 2. 

The impact of taxes which makes it difficult for some small businesses to accumulate working capital or capital for expansion. 

Labor problems, including labor-management relations, the impact of rising labor costs resulting from industry-wide bargaining and from increases in and extension of the minimum wage. 

3. Shrinking margins of profit due to high wage rates, high cost of materials, or lowered volume of business. 

4. Competition from imports. 

5. Competition with large businesses which extend their operations into new fields by diversification or by mergers. 

6. Competition from tax exempt co-operatives. 

7. Inadequacy of management, including inadequate record keeping, inability to engage in market research or product development or unawareness of the need for them, inadequate organization, lack of selling knowhow, and other facets of deficiency in management.

CHAPTER III FINANCIAL ASSISTANCE 

In the period July 1 to December 31, 1957, the Small 

Business Administration approved 1, 501 business loans totaling $73, 362,000, and 767 disaster loans totaling $5, 700, 000. In the full year 1957, the Agency approved 3, 437 business loans totaling $162, 399,000, as compared with 2, 890 approvals for $121, 704, 000 in 1956. In the Disaster Loan Program, in 1957 the Agency approved 2, 086 loans for $16, 061, 000, compared with 1, 531 for $17,743,000 in 1956. 

Since the start of its Financial Assistance Program in September 1953, the Small Business Administration has approved 

8, 597 business loans for $398, 148, 000 and 6, 916 disaster loans for $71, 709, 000 a total of 15, 513 loan approvals for $469, 857, 000. 

In addition to its own lending activities, the Agency is now administering a large number of business and disaster loans which were made by the Reconstruction Finance Corporation and which have been transferred to the Small Business Administration for servicing.

During this reporting period, by Public Law 85-120, approved August 3, 1957, Congress amended the Small Business Act of 1953 to extend the operations of the Small Business Administration through July 31, 1958, and to increase by $75 million the legislative limitation on business loans and commitments outstanding at any one time. The new maximum is $305 million. 

In the Supplemental Appropriation Act of 1958, the Agency's revolving fund for business and disaster loan purposes was increased $100 million to a total of $320 million. 

Administration of Loan Program 

In line with the Agency's desire to reduce loan processing time to the minimum, consistent with thorough analysis, a three-man Loan Review Board has been established in the Washington office. Through the operations of this board and improved coordination with the Agency's field offices, the Agency is now able to act on the majority of loan applications received in 

Washington within 24 hours of their receipt.

The Small Business Administration is now servicing almost 14, 000 business and disaster loans. As the Agency's loan portfolios grow, collection and servicing problems increase. 

A loan administration and liquidation section has been established in each regional office, and these sections are emphasizing financial counseling and preventive action to forestall delinquencies. 

Area work conferences were held in Chicago, San 

Francisco and Atlanta during October and November 1957, and were attended by representatives of the Washington office, local bankers, and business executives. These conferences considered ways to improve program operations involving financial counseling, processing of applications, and loan servicing. 

Financial Counseling 

Small Business Administration financial specialists who have had long experience in credit and financial matters are available to counsel with small business owners and managers on their problems.

These specialists explore carefully each request for financial assistance to see if the small firm's problem can be solved without recourse to Government financial aid. 

Frequently, through helpful suggestions, such as for changes in capitalization of the business, liquidation of outdated or surplus inventory, or modernization of sales methods, the financial specialists are able to help the small business overcome its difficulties without borrowing. Where borrowing apparently is the only solution, the specialists often are able to help the business obtain the loan from private lending sources.

On some occasions, after the Small Business Administration has processed a loan to the stage of closing and disbursement, a bank which originally declined the small concern's request has agreed to make the loan. Such a result, while producing no interest income to the Agency to offset the cost of processing the application, is considered in keeping with the mission of the Small Business Administration. In some instances it is evident that a loan would not be the solution to a businessman's financial problems, or that his application would not meet reasonable credit standards. Although the businessman is advised of the facts. in these cases, it is the Agency's policy not to discourage the filing of any application. Therefore, if the businessman wishes to file his application, the Agency accepts it and gives it earnest consideration. 

Applications and Approvals 

During the latest 6-months period, the Agency received 3, 200 business loan applications 327 fewer than in the preceding 6-months period and 137 fewer than in July to December of fiscal year 1957. This conforms to a pattern of reduced applications for loans in the private banking field.

The table on page 35 summarizes the Agency's business loan activity during the past 2-1/2 fiscal years.

Approvals and Declines 

During this 

6-months period there was a slight decrease in the percentage of loan approvals to total applications acted on. by the chart on page 36. 

This is illustrated

At 52. 4 per cent, the ratio of approvals to applications acted on during this period is higher than the cumulative ratio of approvals (51.4 per cent) since the beginning of the Agency's business lending operations. 

Bank Participation

Sixty-six per cent of the business loans approved during this period were bank participation loans. The ratio of loans of this type to total loans continues to decline; in the previous 6-months period 69. 4 per cent of approved loans were bank participation loans. This drop is attributable almost wholly to a decrease in deferred participation loans.

During the last 2 years, a growing number of banks. have asked the Agency to participate on an immediate rather than a deferred basis. This shift to immediate participation loans may reflect a desire of the banks to use their loan funds to extend credit to as many customers as possible. It also may reflect the relative unprofitability to banks of deferred participation loans in view of the higher interest rates which they may obtain on loans made without Agency participation. With a maximum interest rate of 6 per cent on participation loans, the net to a bank after payment of the Agency's participation fee may be as low as 4 per cent, as contrasted with a return of 6 per cent or more which may be obtained by banks on their own loans. 

Despite the decrease in the percentage of participation loans, loans of this type continue to account for a major part of the loan approvals, and are mutually advantageous to the small business owner, the participating institution, and the Government. From the standpoint of the bank, the participation plan affords an opportunity to make a longer term -- and sometimes a larger loan than would otherwise be possible. Further, it permits the bank to broaden its lending activity while maintaining liquidity, obtaining a fair return on money loaned, and providing increased service to its customers. From the standpoint of the Small Business Administration, the plan reduces loan collection and servicing costs, requires fewer Government employees, lessens the drain on lending funds, and may make possible sale of an entire loan, when seasoned, to the participating bank.

Limited Loan Participations The Limited Loan Participation Program was designed especially to assist small retail, wholesale, and service establishments (other types of business also are eligible). A concern of this type may be unable to pledge as much tangible collateral as is required for regular business loans, but may have a favorable earnings, management and credit record and therefore is deserving of consideration.

In this program, the Small Business Administration will join with a bank in a loan to a small firm up to a maximum of $15,000, or 75 per cent of the loan, whichever is the smaller. During the last 6 months, 30. 1 per cent of the business loans approved by the 

Agency were of this type.

As shown in the table, most of the loans have been to retailers, wholesalers, and service establishments, for whom this program was chiefly designed. During the last 6 months, almost 67 per cent of the loans under this plan were to firms in these lines, as compared with 53 per cent of all Agency business loans to such industries.

Characteristics of Business Loans Approved Size 

The 3,200 business loan applications received in the period July-December 1957 requested a total of $190, 709, 000, indicating an average size of loan requested of $59, 600. This was considerably larger than the $53, 600 average for the preceding period, when 3, 527 applications for $188, 916,000 were received. As to loans approved, the average size this period was $48,900, compared with an average of $46,000 in the first 6 months of 1957. Loans approved on an immediate participation basis were largest in average size in this period, at $53,000, while direct loans and deferred participation loans averaged $47, 500 and $38, 600, respectively.

The largest loan approved during this 6-months period was for $400,000, of which $150,000 was the share of a participating bank, and $250,000 the share of this Agency. The loan was to enable a long-established producer of porcelain enamel products to modernize its equipment, increase its working capital, and consolidate certain obligations. 

The smallest business loan approved during the period was for $1,000. The applicant was a veteran in a Southwestern 

State, who needed working capital to re-open his service station and garage, which he had been forced to close due to illness. 

As shown in the table on page 39, well over half the business loans approved in the period under review were for $25,000 or less, and nearly three-quarters were for $50,000 or less.

Size of Business 

The size pattern of manufacturers receiving loan approvals during the 6-months period July-December 1957 varied only slightly from the first half of the year. During the period under review, 74 per cent of the loans to manufacturers were to companies with fewer than 50 employees; in the preceding period about 76 per cent were to that size class. Only 10 per cent of the loans were to firms with 100 or more employees.

Type of Industry 

The tables on pages 44 and 45 show a breakdown of the business loans approved, by industries, in the period 

July-December 1957, and cumulatively through December 31, 1957. The number of loans to nonmanufacturing industries continues to exceed those to manufacturing industries, partly because of the influence of the Limited Loan Participation Program. However, loan approvals to small manufacturers are high, relative to the number of manufacturers in the total business population.

In retail trade, most loans during the period were to food stores, followed by automotive dealers and filling stations (combined). On a cumulative basis, dealers in building materials, hardware, and farm equipment, led among the retailers, but loans in this group were relatively fewer during the past 6 months.

On a cumulative basis, manufacturers account for 37 per cent of the total number of loans. However, their share of loans during this 6-months period declined to 30 per cent. 

Disbursements and Commitments As of December 31, 1957, disbursements had been initiated on 6, 533 of the 7, 324 net approved business loans after cancellation. This amounted to 89 per cent of net approvals. 

The interval between approval of a loan and disbursement is largely controlled by the borrower, who must take the necessary steps to fulfill the terms and conditions of the loan authorization. Delays sometimes occur because the borrower has difficulty securing acceptable title to real estate, or obtaining delivery of machinery or construction materials, or other reasons. Borrowers are customarily allowed 4 months in which to meet the loan requirements, and extensions of this period may be granted if justified by the circumstances. 

As of December 31, 1957, disbursements made by banks or by the Small Business Administration on business loans

amounted to $287, 111, 000 out of total net commitments of $340, 034, 000. The Agency's share of this net commitment amount was $283, 562, 000.

Cancellations 

Of the 8, 597 business loans approved since start of the Agency's lending program, 1, 273 have been canceled in full prior to disbursement. Cancellations occur primarily for these reasons: 1. 

Borrower made other arrangements for funds; 2. 

Borrower found he could get along without the loan; 3. 

Borrower was unable or unwilling to comply with loan conditions;

Borrower's financial condition deteri- 4. orated to the extent that the loan could not be disbursed.

Repayments 

Of the disbursement total of $287.1 million cited previously, $76.8 million has been credited on the Agency's records, in the form of repayments and other credits (including loans sold and deferred participation agreements terminated), through December 31, 1957, leaving an outstanding balance of $210.3 million at the year end.

Sale of Loans 

In line with its policy of assisting the small business owner to establish and maintain a normal bank-customer relationship, the Small Business Administration offers for sale its seasoned loans. In all cases the borrower's bank of deposit is given the first opportunity to purchase the loan. Including deferred participation loans where the participating bank has taken over the entire loan and thus has relieved the Agency of its liability, there have been 101 loans in the authorized amount of $5,517,000, sold to or refinanced by banks.

Delinquencies, Charge-offs, and Liquidation Activity 

As previously stated, disbursement has been initiated on 6, 533 business loans in an amount of $287, 111,000. Of these, 114 loans were in a liquidation status at December 31, 1957, and 157 had payments due which were delinquent in excess of 60 days. 

At June 30, 1957, 80 loans were in liquidation and 105 loans had payments due which were delinquent more than 60 days. In most instances, adequate collateral has protected the 

Government's interest. Liquidation actions have thus far resulted in the charge-off by Small Business Administration of $152, 000 of principal on 14 loans. This Agency analyzes each delinquent loan in a sympathetic manner, with a view toward assisting the borrower in overcoming his difficulties and at the same time protecting the Government's investment. 

Convalescent and Nursing Home Loans 

The Agency has established a program to assist convalescent and nursing homes in obtaining necessary financing which banks in general have been reluctant to provide partly because the collateral in most cases is highly depreciable and is of a one-purpose nature. Nursing homes have become increasingly important in alleviating the hospital bed shortage by making provision for removal of patients from hospitals and thus providing additional hospital beds for more acute cases. 

Loans will be made for the construction of new facilities, expansion or improvement of existing facilities or for working capital.

Fisheries Loans The Small Business Administration processes, dis burses, and services loans approved by the Secretary of the 

Interior from the Fisheries Loan Fund established under Section 4 of the Fish and Wildlife Act of 1956. Loans may be approved by the Secretary for financing and refinancing of fishing operations; for maintenance, repair and replacement of fishing gear and vessels, and for research into the basic problems of fisheries. Through December 31, 1957, the 

Small Business Administration had processed 351 applications under this program. 

During the last 6 months, 147 applications

were received, of which 63 were approved by the Secretary, 43 were declined, and the remainder had been withdrawn or were pending at the year's end.

These loans have been of substantial assistance to fishermen, enabling them to repair and upgrade their equipment and to liquidate burdensome short-term indebtedness. 

Moreover, in many cases small business concerns in the fishing communities have been aided by the liquidation of their claims against the fishing vessels. 

Pool Loans 

The Small Business Administration was authorized in 1955 to make loans to corporations formed and capitalized by groups of small business concerns for the purpose of producing or securing raw materials or supplies. The loans are limited to $250,000 multiplied by the number of small businesses which have established the corporation. To date, two such pool loan applications have been received and both have been approved. 

It is believed that there are many industries which could use this type of loan with benefit but which have not yet taken advantage of the program. With this in mind, each regional director of the Agency has been asked to determine what industries, if any, in his region, might be assisted through pool loans and to recommend the best ways of keeping them informed of the program. 

Development Credit Corporations 

Development corporations, aside from those organized privately, are of two types: Statewide development credit corporations, and local industrial foundations or local development corporations. Both types are growing in importance as sources of financing for small business, and both are of particular interest to Small Business Administration. The Agency has made loans in participation with State development credit corporations. It has also made loans to local development corporations when the proceeds of the loans are to be used to assist small business firms which would themselves be eligible under the Small Business Act and the Loan Policy Statement.

The Small Business Administration has approved 

16 loans to local development corporations, for a total of $1,364, 000.

Transfer of Reconstruction Finance Corporation Loans Under the President's Reorganization Plan No. 1 of 

1957, the Administrator of the Small Business Administration assumed responsibility on July 1, 1957 for the administration and liquidation of the remaining small business loans of the Reconstruction Finance Corporation. This transfer included 

Reconstruction Finance Corporation "pool" loans serviced by banks, other loans serviced by banks under Loan Servicing Agreements, and loans and receivables formerly serviced by 

Reconstruction Finance Corporation in its Washington office, as well as collateral-purchased items arising from liquidation of loans.

Disaster Loans 

The Small Business Administration is authorized to make low-interest-rate loans to assist in the rehabilitation of homes and businesses which have been damaged or destroyed by floods, storms or other natural disasters. The Agency is also authorized to make loans to businesses which are located in an area designated as a drought disaster area by the President or Secretary of Agriculture and which have suffered economic losses as a result of the drought.

Bank Co-Operation 

Co-operation by private financial institutions in the 

Agency's Disaster Loan Program has been very gratifying. Banks in the disaster areas, or in neighboring towns, have customarily co-operated fully with the Agency's financial specialists, and frequently have furnished personnel to assist applicants in the preparation of credit data and the necessary forms. 

Drought Disaster Loans 

The Small Business Administration provides financial assistance to small business concerns located in drought areas if the concerns have suffered substantial economic injury as a result of the drought condition. Banks in these areas are cooperating with the Agency by accepting drought loan applications

and by obtaining credit information needed in evaluating the loan request. In addition, the Agency has arranged for local advisory committees composed of bankers and businessmen with intimate knowledge of local conditions, to assist in the consideration of applications.

Statutory Limitation on Outstanding Loans and Commitments 

Although appropriations to the revolving fund total only $320 million based on actual and estimated needs through the current fiscal year, the Congress has authorized appropriations for the loan program in a total amount not to exceed $430 million. Of this, not to exceed $305 million in business loans and commitments, and not to exceed $125 million in disaster loans and commitments, may be outstanding at any one time. These limitations relate only to the Government's share of outstanding loans and commitments and do not pertain to the share of participating banks. Thus, the total amount of outstanding loans and commitments might exceed the stipulated amounts. 

As of December 31, 1957, loans and commitments chargeable against the $305. 0 million authorized for the 

Business Loan Program aggregated $221. 1 million, leaving a balance of $83.9 million for new loans. As of the same time, similar charges against the $125 million statutory limitation on disaster loans totaled $48. 8 million, leaving a balance of $76. 2 million for additional loans.

Operating Results of Lending Programs 

As shown in the financial statements in Appendix D, the Agency's lending operations over a period of about 4-1/2 years have resulted in a cumulative deficit of $17, 435, 182, including a loss of $3,230, 232 for the 6-months period ended December 31, 1957. Part of this deficit is attributable to disaster lending operations, and part to business lending. 

Since by statute the maximum interest rate on disaster loans is only 3 per cent, and since interest expense to Treasury on the net amount expended from the revolving fund is payable this fiscal year at a rate of 3-3/4 per cent, it is obvious that the disaster program will show a deficit, even without taking into account administrative expenses and provision for estimated losses.

For some items, such as interest and fee income, separate accounts are maintained by the Agency for the business and disaster lending operations. On the other hand, it would be extremely difficult and expensive to maintain an accounting system which would permit an exact separation of administrative expense costs between the two programs. However, through a combination of accounts and statistics, it is possible to develop a distribution of each income and expense item between the two programs. This distribution, while it must be qualified as partially estimated, is deemed to be reasonably accurate and sufficiently valid for present purposes. 

On this basis, the tabulation on page 58 reflects a distribution of the net loss incurred during the period July 1 through December 31, 1957.

With respect to the table, income is derived from (1) interest on business loans receivable at an average rate slightly below 6 per cent, (2) interest on disaster loans receivable at 3 per cent, and (3) fees on deferred participation loans disbursed by banks at an average rate of approximately 1-1/2 per cent. 

On funds disbursed from the revolving fund, the interest payment to Treasury for the current fiscal year is 3-3/4 per cent. For business loans this leaves a margin of approximately 2-1/4 per cent to partially offset the expense

For of processing applications and closing and servicing loans approved, and the provision for estimated future losses. disaster loans, there is an immediate deficit of 3/4 per cent, without considering expenses and provision for future losses. 

Since no Government funds have been disbursed on deferred participation loans outstanding, the 1-1/2 per cent revenue is available to offset in part the expenses involved in processing applications and closing and servicing the loans approved. 

The provision for estimated losses represents a charge of 1.8 per cent on all new business loans and 5. 4 per cent on all new disaster loans disbursed during the period. 

This provision, which is based on Government lending agency experience factors developed over a period of years, is made in order to accumulate reserves for possible future losses. 

Sufficient experience has not yet been gained to test the validity of these rates with respect to the types of loans made by Small 

Business Administration.

CHAPTER IV GOVERNMENT PROCUREMENT ASSISTANCE 

In the first 6 months of fiscal year 1958 the Small Business Administration, in co-operation with military and Federal civilian agencies, set aside for competitive award to small business concerns 5, 804 Government purchases totaling $378 million. 

This was the greatest number of purchases set aside for small business and the highest dollar amount recorded in the first half of any fiscal year, since the start of the Agency. 

The set-aside totals for this reporting period are made even more impressive by the fact that over-all Government procurement declined so sharply, the dollar amount of Government purchases available for screening by the Agency's representatives was one-fourth less than in the same 6 months of fiscal year 1957. 

Since this is a joint program, this record would not have been possible without the interest and co-operation of the Department of Defense and the other procurement agencies of civilian Departments. Since the start of the Small Business Administration's operations on August 1, 1953, 29, 275 Government purchases totaling $2, 235, 700, 000 have been set aside for exclusive award to small business. Other accomplishments of the Agency in helping small business firms sell to the Government during the past 6 months were: 

1. Small Business Administration field offices reported 8, 580 cases of procurement assistance counseling and representation at purchasing offices in behalf of small concerns, and 3, 300 cases of aid to small firms in obtaining opportunities to bid. 59

2. 

The names of 5, 751 potential small business suppliers of items and services for which additional competition was needed were furnished to Government contracting officers. 

3. Approximately 7,000 small business owners and managers attended the Small Business 

Administration Opportunity Meetings to learn of subcontract and prime contract opportunities, and of the many Government services available to them. 

4. Small Business Administration field special ists made 1, 542 calls on prime contractors to develop subcontracting work for small business firms. As a result of these calls, the Agency referred 1, 981 subcontracting opportunities to small concerns. 

The Joint Set-Aside Program 

To carry out this program, the Small Business Administration has assigned representatives to the major Government purchasing offices. There, in co-operation with procurement officials, they screen proposed purchases to determine whether small firms can supply the needed items or services. When an 

Agency representative finds that a purchase could be supplied by small firms, and that there would be a sufficient number of small business bidders to ensure adequate competition, he recommends that all or part of the purchase be made exclusively from small firms on the basis of competitive bids. If his recommendation is rejected, he has the right to appeal the decision to the highest official of the purchasing office. 

The rules governing set-asides are as follows: When an entire procurement is set aside for exclusive award to small business, any bids received from large business concerns are considered nonresponsive. Where only part of a proposed purchase is set aside for award to small business, small firms must also bid on the unreserved portion of the purchase, that is, the part that is open to all bidders, large and small, in order to be considered in negotiation of a contract for the set-aside portion.

MILLIONS OF DOLLARS 

500

July-Dec: 1954, 1955, 1956 and 1957 400 300 200 $193 $173 100 

8,000 7,000 6,000 5,000 4,000 3.000 2,171 2,000 1,483 1,000 $345 4,789 $378 5,804 JUL-DEC 1954 JUL-DEC 1955 JUL-DEC 1956 JUL-DEC 1957 

SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION 

445240 O-58-5

In addition, a small firm's bid on the unreserved portion must be within 120 per cent of the highest unit price at which the award is made for that portion. In negotiating with small firms that have met these requirements, the purchasing office starts with the one which submitted the lowest bid on the unreserved portion. When only one firm receives an award on the unreserved portion, the set-aside portion must be awarded at the same unit price. When the unreserved portion is obtained through multiple awards, the set-aside portion is awarded at the highest unit price paid for the unreserved portion.

The Small Business Administration set-aside program assures that suitable purchases will be earmarked for exclusive award to small business. Furthermore, when part of a proposed purchase is set aside, the opportunity for small firms to receive awards is doubled; if small firms fail to obtain contracts for the unreserved portion, they still may obtain them for the set-aside portion. Moreover, small firms are given the opportunity to bid or quote on smaller quantities, since a partial set-aside divides the procurement into at least two parts. 

The progress made in setting aside procurements for award to small business is illustrated by the chart on page 61, and the number of contract awards to small business as a result of these set-asides is shown in the chart below. 

The set-aside program also serves to strengthen the national economy inasmuch as it broadens the Government's procurement base by increasing small business participation in NUMBER 9,000

Government contracts. Additionally, the program further implements the declared policy of Congress that a fair proportion of Government contracts be awarded to small business. 

Besides carrying on the set-aside program, Small Business Administration representatives at procurement centers counsel owners and representatives of small business concerns on selling to all Government agencies. They also assist individual small firms by acquainting them with opportunities to quote on various procurements of which they otherwise would be unaware.

Accomplishments in the joint set-aside program are dependent upon the number of purchasing offices which can be covered by Small Business Administration representatives. 

During the past 6 months, 37 Agency representatives have carried on the program regularly in 27 Army purchasing offices, 21 Navy installations, 18 Air Force establishments, 27 Federal civilian agency purchasing offices, and the Military Petroleum 

Supply Agency. 

As manpower would permit, liaison also has been maintained with 74 other purchasing centers which do a lesser volume of purchasing. However, in order to provide the greatest assistance to small firms with the personnel available, the Agency's main effort has been concentrated in the larger purchasing installations.

The table on page 65 shows the results of the joint setaside program in the past 6 months by Department or Agency with which the Small Business Administration has an active program. 

The results of the program with the Department of Commerce have been especially gratifying. Of the procurements screened jointly with that Department, 57 per cent of the number and 35.9 per cent of the dollar amount were set aside for small business concerns.

The purchasing activities of the Departments of Agriculture and the Interior are so widely decentralized that Small Business Administration representatives are able to cover only a small percentage of these agencies' purchasing offices.
