Increasing Small Business Competition 

As a supplement to the set-aside program, Small Business Administration representatives at procurement centers and the Agency's field offices co-operate in locating competent small business suppliers of those items or services for which small business competition previously has been inadequate or lacking. 

The primary aim of this program is to increase the number and variety of items and services on which joint set-asides for small business may be made. This objective is gradually being accomplished. The program also broadens the Government's procurement base and helps to assure a better price to the Government because of greater competition. Even where the recommendation of additional small business suppliers does not result in set-asides for small business concerns, it gets small firms on the proper bidders lists so that they may bid on future procurements. 

Following are some of the items on which the Small Business Administration was able to obtain joint set-asides during the past 6 months by locating competent small business firms desirous of bidding on the purchases: Generator sets, compressors, surgeons' gloves, scouring sponges, mobile field laboratory trailers, metal fabrication, tension guys, boats, protectors (splice), lubricating systems, protractors, dairy products, chute tape, ice cream, jack assembly, metal chairs, adapters, steel tables, food carts, sherbet dishes, medicine cabinets, hand tools, targets, rotary bullet pull parts, steel antenna shelter mounting fixtures, galvanized steel towers, radar support towers, antenna shelter supports, adhesive aluminum dipole, and 36-foot landing crafts.

Procurement Assistance Counseling 

The Small Business Administration helps small firms determine which items or services they can supply to the Government and which agencies buy them, assists the firms in obtaining listing on the appropriate bidders lists, and helps them obtain bid sets for purchases they are able to supply. The Agency is giving particular emphasis to informing small firms interested in doing research and development work how and where to present detailed information about their fields of interest, their research scientists, development engineers and other technical personnel, and their laboratory equipment and research facilities.

In order to provide small firms as full and current information about Government purchasing as possible, the Small Business Administration is revising its "U. S. Government 

Purchasing Directory" and "U. S. Government Specifications 

Directory" and will publish the two in combined form. The new and expanded directory will provide detailed information on the items and services bought by the Federal Government and the agencies which buy them; sources of specifications used by the agencies in their purchasing; steps to take in locating prime contract and subcontract opportunities; and other helpful data. The directory, which is widely used both in industry and Government will be sold by the Superintendent of Documents, United States 

Government Printing Office, Washington 25, D. C. 

Many Small Business Administration field offices display current bid sets for suitable Government purchases and inform interested small firms how copies of the sets and specifications and drawings relating to them can be obtained. 

This Agency and the International Cooperation Administration are collaborating to provide small firms information on export trade opportunities. The International Cooperation Ad ministration furnishes Small Business Administration regional and branch offices "Small Business Circulars" discussing export trade opportunities for small business under the programs financed by the International Cooperation Administration. Small Business 

Administration field offices use these circulars to inform small firms of needed items, quantities, specifications, and so forth. The Small Business Administration also assists small firms in solving problems regarding bids and specifications and helps resolve difficulties between small firms and Government procurement officials. During the 6 months ending December 31, 1957, Small Business Administration field offices reported 8, 580 cases of procurement counseling and representation in behalf of small firms at purchasing offices. 

Research and Development Contracts 

Available statistics indicate that in fiscal year 1957, small business firms received only 4. 3 per cent of the value of military research and development contracts. This low percentage

of small business awards indicates that the scientific knowledge and engineering capacity of small firms are not being utilized to the fullest desirable extent by the Government. 

In research and development procurements, Federal agencies seek participation by the best qualified firms, educational institutions, nonprofit organizations, or individuals in order to receive the finest products or studies. This policy places a premium on experience and on superiority of facilities, so that it is more difficult to set aside for small firms research and development procurements than to set aside supply contracts. 

Small Business Administration field offices counsel and guide small firms and individuals interested in research and development work and direct them to the appropriate procurement officials. The offices also develop additional small business sources for research and development projects when notified by the Agency's representatives at procurement centers that greater small business competition is needed. 

The percentage of research and development contracts awarded to small business firms possibly could be increased by use of the joint set-aside program. Accordingly, the Agency has instructed its representatives at procurement centers to initiate joint set-asides on applied research and development projects when two or more small business firms are considered qualified to carry out the project. This action will give Government procurement officials every opportunity to co-operate in increasing the share of research and development contracts being awarded to small business concerns.

The experience of this Agency shows that much more can be done by this program. A substantially higher dollar value of all research and development procurements screened at the 

Quartermaster Research and Engineering Command at Natick,

Massachusetts, during this 6-months period, were set aside for small business.

Prime Contract Authority 

The Small Business Administration is empowered to enter into contracts with any Department or Agency of the United States Government to furnish supplies and services and to arrange

for the performance of such contracts through subcontracting to small business concerns or others, as may be necessary. 

The Small Business Administration believes that its authority to take prime contracts in order to extend subcontracting should be continued on a stand-by basis for use in an extraordinary emergency.

Business Opportunity Meetings 

During the period of this report, the Small Business Administration, other Federal agencies, and State and local business groups cosponsored 10 Small Business Opportunity Meetings. 

More than 7,000 small business owners and managers attended these meetings and learned first-hand of the Government's buying methods, prime contract and subcontract opportunities, and helpful services available from the Small Business Administration and other Federal agencies.

Opportunity Meetings were held during this period in 

Atlanta, Georgia; Providence, Rhode Island; Fresno, California; 

Denver, Colorado; Richmond, Virginia; Jacksonville, Florida; 

Akron, Ohio; San Diego, California; Topeka, Kansas; and Erie, Pennsylvania.

At the Opportunity Meetings information on how to sell to the Government is presented by means of talks by top Government purchasing officials; visual displays; sample bid sets, and exhibits of products purchased by military and Federal civilian agencies. Each meeting also features exhibits by large prime contractors of items for which subcontractors are being sought. 

In addition, Small Business Administration specialists and Government contracting officers provide on-the-spot procurement counseling and assist individual firms in obtaining listing on appropriate bidders lists.

One of the chief benefits derived from the meetings has been to bring many small subcontractors or suppliers to the attention of interested prime contractors. Both small and large firms have expressed the view that the meetings are a very effective form of prime contract and subcontract assistance to small business.

Defense Mobilization Planning 

The Administrator of the Small Business Administra tion recently has been designated a member of the Defense Mobilization Board. This is further recognition of the essential role of small business in a national emergency and the need for full utilization of the capabilities of small plants in defense mobilization plans.

The Small Business Administration serves as a spokesman for small business concerns in defense mobilization planning, and has had a representative working with the Office of Defense Mobilization for the past year. During the period covered by this report, the Agency, through its liaison with the Office of Defense Mobilization, initiated and developed a program for evaluating the state of the Nation's small business concerns under simulated or actual attack conditions. In this connection, the Agency's field offices are supplying basic data with respect to production facilities of small firms. Approximately 4, 000 small business concerns already have been coded and a goal of 100, 000 is planned. In order to avoid duplication of similar data available through other sources such as the Bureau of the Census, firms of 20-99 employees only are being coded for this purpose. 

Both the Washington and regional offices of the Small 

Business Administration participated in Operation Alert 1957. 

As part of this operation, an Agency representative briefed the 

Director of the Office of Defense Mobilization and the heads of other Government agencies on the results to small business capacities and facilities of simulated attack. 

Prime Contract Referral Program 

When a small business concern requests Small Business Administration assistance in obtaining Government orders and contracts, the Agency secures detailed information regarding its production facilities. The Agency then notifies the firm of current bidding opportunities which are suited to its particular type of equipment. This service is provided until the firm has had time to get on the bidders lists and receive notice of future purchases direct from the purchasing installations. Since the Agency's field offices obtain bid sets from the principal Government purchasing

offices, they are able to notify small firms of many bidding opportunities of which the firms otherwise would be unaware. 

During the past 6 months, 14, 705 notifications of current bidding opportunities were made to small business concerns which had requested such assistance. Statistics on awards resulting from prime contract referrals have been discontinued because of the great amount of time required to obtain the data. However, past data indicate that this program is a very effective aid to small firms. For instance, during the only period for which data are available July 1954 through June 1956 awards to small firms as the result of referrals amounted to $579, 491,000. 

Subcontracting Program 

In its Subcontracting Program, the Small Business Ad ministration has two primary objectives: To help small prime contractors locate needed subcontracting facilities and to develop subcontracting opportunities for interested small firms with the major Government suppliers.

To carry out this program, the Agency compiles data on the facilities of small firms which have asked assistance in obtaining subcontracts. Through use of this data, the Agency is able to recommend capable subcontractors to firms requiring additional production facilities. 

Major prime contractors that have agreed to establish small business subcontracting programs in co-operation with the 

Department of Defense are seen by Small Business Administration representatives to determine component parts which will be subcontracted. Through its facilities records, the Agency is then able to recommend suitable small firms for these subcontracting opportunities. 

The Small Business Administration and the Atomic 

Energy Commission also have established a co-operative subcontract program to aid small firms in obtaining subcontracts from the Atomic Energy Commission's cost-type contractors. 

Small Business Administration representatives had 1, 542 consultations with major prime contractors during the past

72 Sta months in behalf of small subcontractors. As a result, 1,981 subcontracting opportunities were referred to small firms. tistics on contract awards resulting from this program are not available, since the referrals sponsored by the Small Business Administration often result, over a period of years, in multiple contract awards which are not reported. Thus an accurate evaluation of the program is not possible. 

Cases of Procurement Assistance 

Following are illustrations of the wide variety of procurement assistance provided by the Small Business 

Administration: 

After receiving assistance from the Small Business Administration office at Minneapolis since 1955, the proprietor of a small business concern in Minnesota recently wrote: "In our first year we had a total volume of business of $29, 000. 

During fiscal year 1957, our volume will reach $250,000. We were referred to our first four customers through your office, and they are still with us. The amount of work from each has shown an increase each year. Other ways SBA has helped are as follows: Help in preparing Government bids; getting placed on bidders lists; suggestions and advice for development and marketing a new product; other helpful hints we have picked up from time to time by visiting your office. Without SBA assistance we would have closed our doors in our first year of operation. An official of a small San Francisco concern wrote as follows to the Agency office in that city: "We received your letter of December 6, 1957, informing us that . . . payment on our contract would be processed on Monday, December 9, 1957. We attempted to obtain reimbursement for our work for over three and one-half months with no success. You were able to obtain action within ten days from date of the writer's personal appearance in your office with the information concerning the unnecessary delay in our payments A small forging operation in Iowa was completely shut down because of defense contract cutbacks. When the proprietor requested assistance from the Small Business Administration office in Kansas City, he was referred to an Arkansas company

which was seeking a source of forgings. The small concern arranged to supply the needed forgings and now is operating at full capacity.

The staff of the Small Business Administration Chicago office found that requirements and specifications for generator sets should be simplified in order to enable small firms to submit bids. They brought this to the attention of procurement officials and obtained the necessary changes. The Chicago office and several other Small Business Administration offices then cooperated in locating competent small business producers of the various sizes and types of generators who wished to bid on Government contracts. As a result, the Agency has been able to obtain joint set-asides on generator procurements leading to contract awards to eight small business firms totaling approximately $20 million. This is a case in which coordination of three of the 

Agency's procurement assistance programs (specifications assistance, developing potential sources, and joint set-asides) helped small firms sell to the Government. 

An Omaha, Nebraska, firm reports that "As a result of attendance at the Midwest Procurement Conference (Omaha Business Opportunity Meeting) we have been awarded seven contracts for a total of $54, 000, " Through the efforts of the Small Business Administration's New York staff, a small subcontractor on Long Island obtained a 70 per cent partial payment of its termination claim resulting from termination of a Government prime contract. The company had been told by the Government contracting agency that it could not expect payment until the prime contractor's claim was settled, which might take more than a year. Small Business 

Administration assistance in obtaining a partial payment enabled the small business to continue operations without curtailing its working force. After the staff of the Agency's Richmond office had advised him on how to sell to the Government and had referred to him notices of bidding opportunities suitable to his firm's facilities, a North Carolina businessman wrote: "We appreciate the bid invitations we have received in the last several months are now in the midst of Government contracts and . We were the

successful bidder for a contract. which amounted to approximately one-quarter of a million dollars. We . . . welcome the invitations, and want you to know we are appreciative of what your office and the Small Business Administration stand for. " 

Through the assistance given by the Small Business Administration, a small Illinois firm was awarded a contract amounting to $1,800,000. Since the firm was located in a surplus labor area, the entire community was aided by the Agency's efforts.

The proprietor of a small machine shop in Philadelphia visited the Small Business Administration office there to learn of any available bid opportunities in his field. Two current procurements suitable to his facilities were shown to him and arrangements made for him to obtain bidding forms. However, when he received the forms, he had difficulty understanding certain of the phrases and clauses because he was foreign-born and as yet had only a limited knowledge of English. An Agency staff member explained these bidding requirements to him. Subsequently, the proprietor visited the office to say that he had been awarded both contracts due to the assistance given him. 

The proprietor of a small packing firm in Texas recently told the Small Business Administration's Dallas office that a lack of business was forcing him to close his plant. The Dallas staff was able to avert the plant closing by arranging for the small firm to supply 100 sides of beef a day to a member of the Texas 

Meat Packers Association.

The president of a small California firm wrote to the 

Agency's San Francisco office: "Early last month we were awarded a Government contract. . . for the repair, overhaul, and maintenance of material handling equipment. This contract represents quite an extensive step in the growth of our small company. 

The help received from your office was a definite . our being awarded this contract. . . . "I · • factor in A manufacturer of woven fibre hampers asked the Small Business Administration for help in locating a small firm or firms to supply wood dowels needed in its product. The manufacturer had been unable to find a satisfactory replacement for a supplier

who had discontinued business and was itself about to cease operations because of this. Through its field offices, the Small Business Administration was able to supply the names of 16 small companies that wished to bid on the manufacturer's requirements. 

Review of Government Procurement Policies and Procedures 

As a result of a suggestion by the President's Cabinet Committee on Small Business, a task force has been established under the general supervision of the General Services Administration to review procurement policies and procedures in order to increase small business participation in work on Government contracts. Among the committee's goals are to eliminate needless inconsistencies in the procurement procedures of various Departments and Agencies; to simplify the procedures; and to remove from them inequities which hinder small business concerns in their efforts to sell to the Government. 

The Small Business Administration has provided technical assistance and participated in the work of this committee. 

The Agency has assigned a staff member to the task force on a fulltime basis, and other of its officials have served as chairmen or members of various task force study groups. 

The Agency also has contributed to the work of the task force by referring to it typical problems experienced by small firms in selling to the Government and recommended solutions. 

Among the most pressing small business problems submitted to the task force were cases of hardship arising from slow payments and taking of unearned discounts by the Government, a need for speed-up in handling of receiving and inspection reports by purchasing agencies, and a need for better procedures in Government financing of contracts through progress and advance payments. As a result of the task force studies, uniform criteria and procedures for progress and advance payments have been developed. Special invoice certification requirements have been eliminated. All Government agencies have been notified to issue instructions directing the discontinuance of the improper practice of taking unearned discounts. 

Further, the Comptroller General has granted broad authority to contracting agencies to work out business-like billing

and payment procedures similar to commercial practices. The 

Comptroller General also has issued a decision which provides that on freight charges of under $25, receipted freight bills need not be attached to the invoice submitted for payment. Finally, the task force drafted and there was sent to the Congress proposed legislation to improve the opportunities for small business concerns to obtain a fair proportion of Government purchases and contracts.

CHAPTER V PRODUCTION AND MANAGEMENT ASSISTANCE 

During the last 6-months period, the Small Business Administration continued and expanded its programs for providing production and management assistance to small business concerns.

Certificates of Competency 

The Small Business Administration offers what is in effect an appeal procedure for the small firm which faces loss of a Government contract award because the procurement officer believes the firm lacks the necessary financial or productive capacity. Under the Small Business Act, the Agency is authorized to certify that small firms or small business defense production pools are competent, with respect to financial means and productive capacity, to perform specific Government contracts. Once such a certificate is issued by the Agency, the procurement officer is directed under the law to accept this Agency's determination as conclusive, as far as financial and technical capabilities of the applicant are concerned. 

Certificates of competency are issued by the Small 

Business Administration under the following conditions: 

1. 

The procurement officer has determined that the bidder applying for a certificate is the lowest responsive bidder on an advertised purchase or is within price negotiation range on a negotiated purchase. 

2. The procurement officer has questioned the firm's financial ability or productive capacity with respect to the proposed contract. 445240 O-58-6 77

3. 

A comprehensive study of the firm's facilities, manufacturing experience, technical ability, and financial capabilities in dicates to the Small Business Administration that the firm is competent financially and technically to perform the contract.

After a contract is awarded as the result of issuance of a certificate of competency, a Small Business Administration representative follows closely the small firm's performance. 

If it experiences production or other difficulties, the Agency assists with the problems. 

During the last 6 months, 92 applications for certificates of competency were received by the Small Business Administration and 40 certificates were issued. The table on page 79 shows the number of applications received, the purchasing agency involved and the number of certificates issued since start of the Agency. cases:

Here are a few examples of certificate of competency 

1. A small company in New York City bid on a Government purchase of duplicating paper. The bid was rejected. 

The small firm then applied for and received a certificate of competency, covering the contract, from the Small Business Administration, As a result, the firm was awarded the contract, valued at more than $100,000. The contract was completed ahead of schedule. 

2. A small business concern, already at work on a 

Government purchase of flight jackets, was rejected for a contract for additional jackets on the basis of insufficient productive capacity. Upon request by the firm, the Small Business 

Administration studied its facilities and determined that the manufacturer could meet the requirements of both contracts. 

A certificate of competency was issued by the Agency and the manufacturer was awarded the contract. The contractor completed all deliveries in 150 days, although allowed 210 days by the contract.

3. A small firm with an established reputation for making quality electrical instruments applied for a certificate of competency after a contracting officer questioned its ability to carry out a proposed contract. In reviewing the case, the 

Small Business Administration found that the concern was not meeting delivery commitments on current contracts with defense agencies. The Agency convinced the management of the serious ness of the situation and brought about improved plant practices with closer production management controls. A certificate of competency was then issued, the company received the contract, and the contract was completed ahead of schedule. 

Products Assistance Program 

Through this program the Small Business Administration helps small concerns gain access to information on new products and processes available from Government and industry. 

The program has these two primary objectives: 1. 2. 

To help small concerns obtain data on research and on product, process, and market development needed in solving day-to-day operating problems and in planning new product, production, and marketing programs.

To make available to small firms information on patented products and processes which are of possible use to them in their present operations or in diversifying and expanding their operations.

Both Government and industry have greatly increased their expenditures on research and development in recent years. 

Much of the resulting data, and particularly that which has been developed in Government laboratories and installations, is nonconfidential in nature and is available to interested small business concerns. However, it is not always readily accessible, particularly to small business owners who either do not have the time or do not know where to go for the information needed in solving a specific problem. The Small Business Administration's

Products Assistance Program helps to bridge this gap between the small business concern and the sources of helpful data, both in Government and private industry. 

When a small business concern submits a problem to the Small Business Administration, the Agency obtains recommended solutions from the best qualified sources in indus try and the Government. The solutions suggested by these sources are consolidated by the Agency into a basic report for the inquirer.

The Small Business Administration issues each month a Products List Circular presenting a brief digest of about 100 patented machines, devices, or processes which are available for use by small firms through purchase, licensing, or other commercial arrangement. Included in the circular are both privately-owned and Government-owned patents. 

During the past 6 months 3,311 firms requested and were furnished information on various phases of product, process, and market development. Since the service was established in July 1954, approximately 42, 000 firms have been assisted.

Approximately 8, 000 small concerns have now asked to receive the Products List Circular on a continuing basis. 

During the past year, 3, 357 requests were received from small firms for further information on the 1, 399 patented products and processes listed in the circular.

Technical Production Assistance 

In many cases, small business concerns do not have employees with the specialized technical experience needed to solve production problems. When small concerns are undertaking defense contracts, or are entering fields that differ from their normal production, these problems frequently increase. 

Small Business Administration field staff specialists are available to help the firms in solving their difficulties. These specialists possess professional backgrounds in engineering, production, materials, specifications, and other areas of the industrial and general production fields.

Frequently, small firms are assisted in acquiring basic materials and production equipment, or are guided on plant lay-out, methods engineering, and related problems. 

Competition with private engineering and related firms is avoided by the Agency's staff members. When an 

Agency production or industrial specialist finds that a small company's problems are broad enough to require the attention of a professional consultant, he advises the company of this. 

However, many problems which small firms bring to the Agency do not require the services of a professional consultant. 

Materials and Equipment Assistance 

During the last 6 months, steel became more readily available to small firms because of additional production from new or enlarged facilities and reduced needs of large consumers. 

However, there were occasional spot shortages of heavy steel plate and heavy structural shapes needed by small firms. 

Some small electroplaters were unable to obtain all needed supplies of nickel anodes during the early months of this period, but by November the supply apparently was sufficient. 

The supply of new machine tools now seems greater than the demand. However, small firms faced with the high cost of new production equipment, frequently ask the Agency for help in locating commercial sources of used equipment or sur plus Government-owned equipment. 

The Agency also carries on these functions on behalf of small business: 1. 

The Office of Defense Mobilization asks the Small Business Administration to review each request from the Department of Defense for approval to lease Government-owned production equipment for nondefense use, to see that such leases do not adversely affect the competitive position of small business.

2. 

The Small Business Administration serves as the representative of small business with other Government agencies which are considering the adoption or revision of materials and equipment policies, programs, or regulations affecting small firms. 

Through its regional and branch offices, the Agency provides small firms information on the Government's materials and equipment regulations.

During the period covered by this report Small Business Administration representatives participated in the work of the 

Inter-Agency Task Group on Uniform Leasing Practices, which developed uniform leasing rates for Government-owned tools.

Inventory of Small Plant Facilities The Small Business Administration's Facilities Inventory is a nationwide program for compiling data on the production facilities of small firms. Small firms are asked to register with the 

Agency's field offices, which are responsible for maintaining the inventory.

The inventory is used (1) to assist registered firms in obtaining prime contract and subcontract opportunities, (2) to furnish additional bidders to Government contracting offices, (3) to locate specialized or scarce machine tools and equipment, and (4) to provide data for defense mobilization planning. Increasing attention is being given to listing companies interested in research and development work. 

As of December 31, 1957, there were 36, 945 small companies registered with the Agency. 

Rural Development Program The Administrator of the Small Business Administration is a member of the Inter-Departmental Rural Development Program 

Committee of the United States Department of Agriculture, and

Agency representatives participate in the work of the Committee and local rural development committees. 

The Small Business Administration field staff has been especially active in the work done in pilot county areas. By assisting small business concerns with their technical and financial problems, they have sought to increase the off-the-farm employment opportunities for farm family members of these areas.

For example, Agency production specialists surveyed a feed mill in a small Southwestern community and found that the management had the prospective business and management ability to operate a plant twice the capacity of the existing one. The 

Small Business Administration then participated in a loan to the mill for expansion purposes. As a result of the increased business, plant employment rose from 8 to 20 persons. 

Surplus Labor Area Assistance 

Small Business Administration assistance to surplus labor and underdeveloped areas stresses the "building from within" approach, that is, emphasis is given to helping existing small firms to expand and to diversify. This approach serves to supplement the area development program of the United States Department of Commerce, which provides information on how to bring new industries into these areas. 

In their work in these areas, Small Business Administration production and industrial specialists meet with the managers of small business concerns and with community leaders to consider means of stabilizing or expanding local employment. 

Consideration is given to the possibility of expanding the market for present products, introducing additional or new products, or obtaining Government prime contracts or subcontracts. 

In response to official requests, the Small Business Administration staff specialists have studied the industrial potential in several labor surplus areas. In a recent survey, the 

Agency and a State Resources and Development Commission took steps to bring about closer co-operation, and use of advisory services of the State engineering college and of retired industry

This type of people who could help small business managers. arrangement will make possible greater assistance to small business concerns in given areas. 

Tax Amortization Assistance 

The rapid tax amortization program to spur construction of emergency facilities is still in operation. Since 1955 each application, large or small, has been reviewed on its own merits and certificates granted until the desired capacity has been reached. 

Under Public Law 85-165, dated August 26, 1957,

Congress amended the Act by greatly reducing the authority of the Office of Defense Mobilization to issue certificates for rapid tax amortization. As a result, very few applications for tax amortization certificates were considered by the Office of Defense Mobilization and the delegate agencies during the period of 

August 26, 1957, through December 31, 1957. During the period when new goals were under consideration, the Small Business Administration participated on the Ad Hoc Committee on Expansion Goals. Since only a limited number of applications were recently considered under the program, there was little activity in the Small Business Administration's two tax amortization functions. These functions are: 1. 2. 

To obtain additional information about small firms' applications for tax amortization certificates when they lack essential data; and

To check for small firms on the status of their applications. 

As of December 31, 1957, four expansion goals remained

However, only one was for a type of facility which small concerns are likely to construct. open.

Specifications Assistance 

The Agency has continued its efforts to overcome the problems of an inadequate supply of Government specifications to meet the needs of small firms. However, many small firms

still receive bid sets from Government contracting officers without the appropriate or complete specifications and then, when they request the specifications, find that the supply has been exhausted.

The Agency maintains at its Washington and field offices reference sets of the packaging and packing specifications most often required by small concerns in selling their products and services to the Government. 

The Agency also has continued to work closely with the 

Task Force for Reviewing Government Procurement Policies and 

Procedures. Technical guidance was provided the Task Force study groups seeking (1) to simplify Government marking, packing and packaging specifications and (2) to determine whether small business suffers a disadvantage in obtaining use of Governmentowned facilities. In addition, an Agency staff member served on the study group dealing with the over-all subject of Government specifications. The Agency has submitted a number of subjects to the study group for consideration and possible solution. A major revision of the "U. S. Government Specifications 

Directory" is nearing completion. This Directory is a guide to approximately 900 places throughout the country where small business owners may refer to Government specifications and indexes. 

In a

During this reporting period, the Small Business Administration reviewed 133 new or proposed interim Federal specifications to make certain they would in no way restrict the opportunities for small firms to sell to the Government. number of instances, the Agency recommended to the General Services Administration the deletion of provisions which might have been harmful to small business. The Small Business Administration also presses for issuance of new specifications, or revision of existing ones when this will increase the opportunities for small manufacturers to have their products considered in Government purchasing. Negotiations toward this end have been conducted recently with the Department of Agriculture, Quartermaster Corps, Marine Corps, Coast Guard, Signal Corps and the General Services Administration.

Small Business Production Pools for Defense 

The Small Business Administration is authorized to advise and assist in the formation of small business production pools and to formally approve the pools' proposed operations, subject to the concurrence of the Attorney General and the 

Chairman of the Federal Trade Commission. 

The principle behind production pools is the belief that many small businesses will be assisted in obtaining contracts and orders for the production of items and services which would not otherwise be obtained and which in all probability they would not seek. Experience indicates that pools are less likely to be successful during a period of defense mobilization than in wartime, and interest in production pools has declined steadily since the close of World War II. As of December 31, 1957, no applications for approval of new pools were pending. Of the five approved pools, only one considered itself still active and was seeking defense prime and subcontracts. The other pools were maintaining their organizations on a stand-by basis. 

Two of the five approved pools have reported receiving 

283 prime contract awards, totaling $1, 103, 472, and 4 subcontract awards valued at $85, 967.

Management Courses 

The administrative management course program, designed to improve the management skills of small business proprietors, continues to be well received both by small business executives and by the educational institutions which cosponsor courses with the Small Business Administration. Fiftythree courses were cosponsored during the last 6 months of 1957. 

These courses were conducted by 45 educational institutions and were attended by approximately 1, 500 owners and managers of small businesses. Since the beginning of the program about 3-1/2 years ago, 10, 270 small business executives have completed or are now participating in the courses. Thus far, a total of 317 courses have been conducted in 114 educational institutions.

In the sponsorship of a course, the educational institution assumes full educational and financial responsibility, while the Small Business Administration helps to promote the course, suggests speakers, and serves as a clearinghouse for ideas concerning subject matter, teaching methods, and administrative techniques. Frequently, the educational institution and the Agency have the assistance of local business organizations and groups. Typically, a course runs for 8 weeks, 1 evening a week for 2-1/2 hours. The subjects covered relate to administration planning, organizing, staffing, directing, controlling rather than day-to-day operations of a business. 

To strengthen the program the Agency is working closely with the National Council for Small Business Management Development. This Council was organized to stimulate management development programs for small business, encourage research in all areas pertinent to small business management, and provide a means for exchanging ideas among members. Its membership consists of persons active in the improvement of small business management and educators located throughout the Nation with experience in conducting administrative management courses cosponsored by the Small 

Business Administration. 

The following is a list of the schools and cities in which administrative management courses cosponsored by the Small 

Business Administration have been held:

Management Counseling 

By means of its counseling program, the Small Business Administration provides assistance to small business owners and managers on a personal, face-to-face basis. This service is growing in popularity with businessmen who prefer to "talk out" a problem rather than to write a long letter detailing it. 

The Agency's counseling assistance ranges from help to those who are considering starting their own businesses to answering inquiries from experienced business proprietors who want to learn of the most advanced business practices and techniques. 

Recent inquiries from prospective business operators have asked for information on starting such firms as a mail order business, a self-service laundry, a truck rental business, a typing and multilith offset business, an ice skating rink, a metal plating shop, a house wrecking concern and a book publishing firm. 

Owners of new businesses ask the Small Business

Administration for information on such subjects as disposal of metal scrap, comparative operating figures, analysis of costs, leasing production equipment, budgeting, and sales analysis. 

More experienced businessmen request information on such subjects as cash discounts, credit policies, techniques of 445240 O-58-7

marketing research, wage incentives for salespeople, and ways to meet changes in consumer demand and the movement of population.

In order to be certain that the latest information is constantly available to these inquirers, the Small Business Administration has established a reference library in the Washington office and is setting up smaller libraries in each of the regional offices. These libraries contain references, text books, booklets which have been publicly and privately printed, business papers, business magazines, and other helpful publications. The libraries are open to all businessmen and students of small business. 

Management Publications The Small Business Administration's management publications continue to grow in reputation as a source of practical information for owners and managers of small concerns. 

Evidence of this fact is the steadily increasing distribution and sale of these publications. Not only do small business owners and managers study and use the publications, after receiving them from larger companies with which they have dealings, but they are used also by university teachers of business subjects as class reference material; trade associations supply them to members; private publishers review and quote them; and professional personnel serving small business -- public accountants, consultants, lawyers, and the like -- use them in advising clients.

The Agency issues five series of management publica tions: Management Aids for Small Manufacturers, Technical Aids for Small Manufacturers, Small Marketers Aids, the Small Business Management Series, and the Aids Annuals. The three types of Aids are distributed free on request, while the Small Business Management Series and Annual volumes are sold by the Superintendent of Documents at nominal prices. 

Management Aids for Small Manufacturers 

This series, in which 12 publications are issued each year, is designed to supply needed information on sound business administration in small plants. To date, 89 Aids have been published. The Aids issued during this reporting period are as follows:

"Key Factors in Starting a New Plant" "Analyzing Your Cost of Marketing" 

"Making Your Sales Meetings Profitable" 

"Traps to Avoid in Small Business Management" 

"Improving Foreman Relations in Small Plants" 

"Job Evaluation in Small Industry" 

As of December 31, 1957, over 2, 300, 000 Management

Aids had been distributed by the Agency on request. 

Of these, 224,000 copies were distributed during the last 6 months.

Technical Aids for Small Manufacturers 

This series, also on a 12-issues-per-year schedule, presents information on significant developments in such fields as equipment, maintenance, materials, and processes. To date, 57 Aids have been published. Those issued during this 6-months period are: "Surface Hardening Practices" "Increase Production with Ceramic Cutting Tools" 

"Uses of Ceramic Coated Metals in Small Plants" "Should Your Plant Lease Production Equipment" 

As of December 31, 1957, 793, 475 Technical Aids had been distributed on request, including 85, 275 copies distributed during the last 6 months.

Small Marketers Aids 

Through these publications, the Small Business Administration seeks to improve management of small retail, wholesale, and service firms. To date, 29 Aids have been published on a 

The most recent publications are: 

12-per-year basis. "Individuality in Retail Advertising" "Small Business and the Federal Trade Commission" "Are You Kidding Yourself About Profits" 

"Stock Management in Small Stores'' "Public Relations for Small Business Owners" 

"Profitable Buying for Small Retailers" "Basic Accounting for Small Partnerships"

As of December 31, 1957, over 769,000 Marketers

Aids had been distributed on request. Of these, 229, 000 copies were distributed during the last 6 months.

Small Business Management Series 

This is a series of larger, more detailed publications than the Aids. Manuscripts normally are prepared under contract by recognized authorities in the fields covered. Texts run from about 5,000 to 60,000 words and the booklets are sold by the Government Printing Office at prices ranging from 15 to 35 cents. 

The most recent booklet, Ratio Analysis for Small 

Business, is No. 20 in the series and was released in July 1957. 

More than 23,000 copies of the booklet have been sold by the 

Government Printing Office. 

As of December 31, 1957, a total of 563, 374 booklets had been sold, including 51, 132 copies purchased by small business owners and others during the last 6 months. 

Aids Annuals 

Through this series, the Small Business Administration makes back numbers of the Aids available in permanent, bound form. Each winter the Aids released 2 years before are revised and reprinted as a book. Copies are sold through the Superintendent of Documents. To date, four volumes have appeared: 

Management Aids for Small Business: Annual No. 1, No. 2, and No. 3; and Technical Aids for Small Business: Annual No. 1. 

As of December 31, 1957, a total of 60, 210 Annuals had been sold. Of these, almost 10,000 copies were sold during the last 6 months.

President's Small Business Conference 

A highlight of the Federal Government's small business assistance programs during this period was the holding of the first 

President's Conference on Technical and Distribution Research for the Benefit of Small Business.

The meeting was called by President Eisenhower for September 23 through 25, 1957, in Washington, D.C., and was organized and arranged by a committee headed by private citizens. 

The chairman was Dr. Eric A. Walker, president of the Pennsylvania State University. Invitations to the conference were extended to small business owners and to persons from various other fields, both in private industry and the Government, in order to bring to bear well-rounded experience upon the problems under discussion.

Prior to formulating the program, two private research agencies were commissioned to determine by interview with individual businessmen, what their problems were in the fields of technical and distribution research. A substantial sample of operators of smaller firms were interviewed in selected cities by representatives of two research organizations. 

Over 25 owners of small business presented their own case histories to those in attendance, indicating in most instances how they met specific management problems as they arose in their own business. Some 30 work sessions which provided the basis for the conference recommendations were held on more important technical and distribution research topics of current interest. 

On October 17, 1957, Sinclair Weeks, Secretary of 

Commerce, and Wendell B. Barnes, Administrator, Small

Business Administration, submitted to President Eisenhower a report on the conference containing six recommendations.

These recommendations, and the action taken thus far on them, are as follows: 

1. The Department of Commerce and the Small 

Business Administration, utilizing their field facilities, should co-operate fully with chambers of commerce, trade associations, development organizations, universities, State and local governments and other appropriate groups in the organization and conduct of similar conferences at the regional, State or local level. 

On November 8, 1957, instructions to carry out this recommendation were sent jointly to the field offices of the two Federal agencies. By the end of the

calendar year, a date had been set for a conference in St. Louis and tentative dates had been decided upon for conferences in Cincinnati, Oklahoma City, and 

Corvallis, Oregon. Other conferences are in the planning stage. 

2. The Department of Commerce and the Small 

Business Administration should take steps jointly to promote and facilitate the dissemination of technical and distribution research materials to small businesses through trade associations, the trade and technical press and larger companies. 

Both Federal agencies are strengthening their relationships with these groups. The Small Business 

Administration is now working with a growing number of large corporations which have customer relation programs and are interested in improving the management skills of their business customers. 

3. The Federal Government should convene a meeting of representatives of colleges, universities and research bureaus to discuss ways of increasing and improving the services which educational institutions provide for small businesses in the fields of technical and distribution research and in business management generally.

The Small Business Administration, which is working closely with over 100 educational institutions in its Administrative Management Course program, is arranging for representatives of the Department of 

Commerce and the Small Business Administration to meet with representatives of collegiate schools of business to discuss such topics as increased distribution and management research, management training and counseling for owners of smaller firms, and job opportunities in small business for college graduates. 

A similar series of meetings with officials of engineering colleges, with emphasis given to technical research subjects, is planned for a later date. 4.

The Federal Government should consider calling a second national conference for the benefit of small business,

to be organized and conducted at a suitable time in the same manner as the recent conference. Special emphasis should be given in such a conference to the management problems of small business, including, but not confined to, the problems of technical and distribution research. 

A second national conference is anticipated.

No date or over-all topic has been decided upon, primarily because the regional and State conference program has not yet been completed. 

5. Federal procurement agencies should be asked to re-examine their policies and procedures with the object of increasing the participation of small companies in research and development contracts.

At the direction of the White House, the 

Honorable Franklin G. Floete, Administrator,

General Services Administration, has been asked to refer this recommendation to the Task Force for 

Reviewing of Government Procurement Policies and 

Procedures.

This Task Force was established in accordance with a recommendation of the President's 

Cabinet Committee on Small Business. 

6. 

Federal Government agencies issuing publications dealing with technical and distribution research should be urged to give greater emphasis in the preparation of these publications to readability and to the commercial application of the subject matter.

The Small Business Administration has written to all Federal agencies which issue publications of interest to small business, asking that they bear in mind this recommendation when preparing their publications. Most of the agencies already have assured the Small Business Administration of their full co-operation.

APPENDIX A ORGANIZATION OF SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION 

The management of the Small Business Administration is vested in an Administrator, who is appointed by the President, by and with the advice and consent of the Senate, and three Deputy Administrators, who are appointed by the Administrator to assist him in carrying out the functions of the Agency. The act which established the Agency, the Small Business Act of 1953, as amended, also provides for a Loan Policy Board consisting of the Administrator, as chairman, the Secretary of the Treasury, and the

Secretary of Commerce. The Board is charged with establishing broad general policies for the granting and denial of applications for financial assistance. 

The Deputy Administrator for Administration directs the administrative operations of the Small Business Administration. He recommends policies and gives general guidance to the Offices of the Controller, Personnel, Program Analysis and 

Organization and Management. 

The Deputy Administrator for Financial Assistance directs the lending and financial counseling programs of the Small Business Administration. He recommends policies and gives general guidance to the Office of Financial Assistance. 

The Deputy Administrator for Procurement and

Technical Assistance directs the procurement, production, managerial, products and technical assistance programs. He negotiates with other agencies on proposed programs and policies, and gives general guidance to the Office of Procurement and Technical Assistance.

The General Counsel is responsible to the Administrator for all legal work concerning the Small Business Administration. 

In carrying out this responsibility he is assisted by the legal staff assigned to the Office of the General Counsel. 101

The Director of the Office of Information is responsible for directing the preparation and issuance of current releases, background statements, pamphlets and other informational materials to the public and to small business concerns. 

The Economic Adviser is responsible for directing and coordinating economic policy for the Small Business Administration. 

He conducts studies and evaluations of business financing, the tax structure, business size criteria, and Government controls and regulations.

National Advisory Organization 

The National Council of Consultants serves as an advisory group to the Administrator in developing, evaluating, and carrying out Small Business Administration programs. The membership consists of outstanding persons, chosen from all parts of the Nation, who represent small business or have a knowledge of and interest in its problems.

Agency Field Offices The Small Business Administration has 15 regional offices, which are located in principal cities throughout the United States and which are under the direct jurisdiction of the Administrator. Directors of these offices are responsible for carrying out, under broad delegations of authority, all Small Business Administration programs within their respective geographic areas, including the direction and supervision of subordinate field offices. 

Within the regions are 37 subordinate field offices. These are located in key cities in the United States, the territories of 

Alaska and Hawaii, and the Commonwealth of Puerto Rico. These offices have specific operating responsibilities which they carry out under delegation of authority from the respective regional directors. Their operations include processing and approving loans and giving procurement and technical aid to small business firms. 

Where necessary, temporary disaster loan field offices are established in areas designated as disaster areas by the Administrator.

Regional Advisory Organizations man. 

The National Board of Field Advisers consist of 15 Regional Advisory Boards, each of which meets with the Small Business Administration regional director, who serves as chair- These boards advise the regional directors in developing, carrying out, and evaluating Small Business Administration programs. The Board members are predominantly businessmen engaged in the operation of small businesses within the respective regions.
